		Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.	
Wellness
I spoke with Dr. Sumbul Desai, Apple’s VP of Health and Fitness, to learn more about the redesigned Health app and its new features.
Senior Editor, Health and Home
Senior Editor, Health and Home
September 18, 2026, 1:01 pm ET
Last week at Apple’s “Surprise and Shine” event, Dr. Sumbul Desai, Apple’s VP of Health and Fitness, announced the redesigned Health app and its fresh features. Between the new Insights tab with Apple Intelligence-powered suggestions, a Longevity tab that shows your Health Age, a readiness score, movement assessments, a blood panel and curated doctor videos, my head was swimming with questions. The following day, I sat down for a video chat with Desai for deeper insight into Apple’s reimagined health platform.
On Wednesday, the new Health app debuted in the first developer beta of iOS 27.2. Some announced features will be available in later betas, including personalized summaries under the Insights tab, personalized recommendations and the ability to order a blood panel from Quest Diagnostics. 
“Features will roll out progressively,” Apple said in an email. “Feedback during the beta period will help us continue refining it ahead of the public release in late 2026.”
Here’s everything you can expect to uncover in the Health app and the features you shouldn’t sleep on.
Launched in 2014, the Health app was initially intended to be like a spreadsheet, with data flowing in, Desai explained. However, the team realized they wanted more from the app, so it evolved over the past 12 years into what Desai now calls “an experience.”
Those at the helm of the Health app’s 2026 makeover considered how to leverage Apple’s machine learning and AI to help people live healthier, while also personalizing the app’s recommendations and turning it into an intelligent hub, not unlike the iPhone itself. For instance, as you open the app and move through your day, different screens appear to reflect your health status. 
Movement assessments have also been added to provide an even more complete picture of a user’s health and track progress or decline. Desai described them as a “mini physical” you can complete at home.
All assessments under the app’s Longevity tab are science-based and contribute to a rainbow-petal visual that illuminates your state of health. Some assessments rely on vision-based AI models that integrate the phone’s camera, marking the first time Apple has used the camera for health.  
The current iteration of the Health app is just the beginning and was designed to set in motion the next era of the team’s work in health and fitness, as Desai said those working on the app still feel they have more to do.
One standout feature from Apple’s event was the Health app’s Longevity tab, which will include a feature called Health Age that uses health metrics such as your VO2 max (the maximum amount of oxygen your body consumes during exercise), resting heart rate, sleep and heart rate variability, along with lab data such as LDL cholesterol and A1c for your average blood sugar level, to provide your biological age, which represents how your body is aging on a cellular level when compared to your actual age. 
Apple’s own Heart and Movement Study, which it applies across all its health products, informs both Health Age and Readiness, which uses your activity data, daytime and overnight vitals and sleep data to tell you about your capacity for the day ahead. 
The five-year Heart and Movement Study was conducted in collaboration with the American Heart Association and Brigham and Women’s Hospital to explore the connection between heart health and physical activity. Apple used that data to create the normative ranges for the insights it provides in the app.
While Desai acknowledged the Health Age assessment is more of a “fun way to look at your health habits” and that no one can tell you your actual biological age, it’s another way to provide insight and suggest healthy habits users can integrate into their lives. 
Apple isn’t the first company to offer features such as a readiness score or biological age, but Desai said that it stands out from competitors because of its unique perspectives, experiences and the science behind its work. 
“When we first started our work in health, when we announced our irregular heart rhythm notifications and ECG, those were clinically validated scientific features,” she said. Desai likened those early days to building a muscle that lets the team create impactful products the medical community will appreciate, and users can easily understand.
That muscle Apple first built has been exercised with all the Health app’s new features, as Desai said that everything is rooted in science. On her team, for instance, physical therapists graded each movement in the app’s mobility assessments to ensure that when a movement is picked up and a result is given, it’s graded the way an actual physical therapist would grade it. 
Uniting science and expertise, Desai said that’s what Apple brings to the table, and its Health app experience. 
Much of the discourse following Apple’s event has been about the new readiness score and Health Age. I was curious what Desai felt was the “sleeper hit” feature that didn’t get as much attention. 
“It’s actually the assessments,” Desai said. “You’re going to be able to, on a regular basis, do a push-up test and a core test and see if you’re improving. Then we give you insights and things that you can do to improve, and then you can test again. It’s almost like having a physical therapist or a trainer.”
The VO2 max test, in particular, marks the first time Apple’s doing an on-demand, in-the-moment VO2 max test. 
You can then take all your assessment results, and the Health app will put them into a report you can share with your doctor. 
Based on its new assessments, the Longevity tab’s visualization has seven tabs: heart health, sleep, mental wellbeing, movement health, metabolic health, hearing and nutrition. But how did Apple narrow it down to those seven?
“We picked areas where we felt like we had the appropriate data to comment on,” Desai explained. “For example, we do a cardiovascular risk score through some of the questions. It’s based on traditional risk scores and cardiovascular risk scores that are done.”
The team prioritized information they believe people will use and assessments they’ll complete, while ensuring the chosen health areas feel holistic and will help people concerned about long-term quality of life.
“There’s still more that we think we can do, but this is where we’re starting,” Desai stated. In other words, Longevity’s rainbow visual could gain more colors in the future. 
When it comes to its Health app, Desai said privacy is where they start and end. 
“Candidly, we may not always be the first to market, but when we release something, we can stand behind it from a privacy standpoint,” Desai stated. “All the features you saw [announced] are no different. … It’s part of the conversation every day, and up till the day that the products are launched.”
Apple will hold off on releasing a product if it hasn’t met its privacy bar. The team behind the Health app also uses a checks-and-balances system, having other teams review their work to ensure they are nailing their privacy approach. 
“In this world where more things happen in the cloud, we want to make sure that’s all encrypted and private, which is why we have Private Cloud Compute,” said Desai. Private Cloud Compute is Apple’s cloud intelligence system designed for private AI processing.
The team’s approach is end-to-end because privacy matters for all your data, not just health data. 
The Health app’s new Insights tab uses Apple Intelligence to analyze your health metrics and provide a timely summary that updates throughout the day. 
“We also make suggestions,” said Desai. “For example, if you have an injury or you’re trying to work on your mobility, we’ll give you suggestions on the types of exercises you might be doing.”
It’s another example of how the app is evolving from a database to something that more closely resembles a personalized health advisor.
The daily insights are meant to be bite-size pieces of information that are one small thing you can do for your health each day. Some days these insights may be interesting; other days, you might be given information you already know.
For $119, the new Health app will let you purchase a blood panel with over 50 biomarkers through Quest Diagnostics. You can also get your blood pressure, height, weight and waist and hip circumferences measured. All this helps provide a more complete picture of metabolic health.
The revamped app will include videos from doctors that help people better understand what they see in the app. 
“When you get your assessment results, you’ll actually see your own data in the videos where they’re explained by clinicians,” said Desai. “We have a physician, a physical therapist, a sleep expert, a mental health expert. The reason we did that is, for certain things, gamification works, but for certain things, you just want to know the information in an easy-to-understand way from an authoritative resource.”
While an expert explains the subject at hand, your health data, whether from your labs or your Apple Watch, will be overlaid on the video so you can see it as you learn more. 
Ultimately, Desai doesn’t see the future of the Health app as one size fits all. Depending on the feature, the team always chooses what makes health as accessible and understandable as possible. In a world with an abundance of health data, perhaps that’s what matters most: ensuring people understand the numbers they see on their devices, and knowing what to do with them once the screen goes black.

66,2026-09-18,"Micron just pulled off a server memory breakthrough that has both AMD and Intel on board, but the revenue from it sits two fiscal years away while the stock already trades near all-time highs. Here is what that gap means…

Micron (NASDAQ:MU | MU Price Prediction) demonstrated a 512GB server memory module that holds as much memory as four 128GB modules, and says AMD and Intel have validated it for future platforms. That is a genuine engineering step, but the investing tension is simple.
Volume production is expected in the second half of 2027, so nothing in this launch touches Micron’s fiscal 2026 pricing or the fiscal 2027 estimates already published. MU stock closed at $977.50 Thursday after gaining 5.5% on the session, extending a 511.97% one-year rally. Density likely commands some premium, but much of it may simply reshuffle what customers already buy from Micron.
The module fits the AI-server thesis already powering current results. Fiscal Q3 2026 revenue reached $41.46 billion, up 345.7% year over year, at a non-GAAP gross margin of 84.9%.
CEO Sanjay Mehrotra told investors that “DRAM and NAND industry demand continues to significantly exceed industry supply” and that tightness should persist beyond calendar 2027.
Even so, revenue from this specific module cannot land until qualification, yield ramp, and contract pricing arrive in 2027. Investors should watch each checkpoint rather than treat platform validation as a purchase order.
NVIDIA (NASDAQ:NVDA) sits on the demand side of the same trade, consuming Micron’s HBM4 in AI accelerator platforms. Micron said HBM4 has already crossed $1 billion in revenue, with the 12-high ramp running twice as fast as HBM3E.
Western Digital (NASDAQ:WDC) rides the same data-center build-out through hard drives, but no longer plays in NAND after the SanDisk (NASDAQ:SNDK) spinoff. That leaves Micron uniquely exposed to both DRAM and NAND pricing (we profiled seven other suppliers riding the same AI infrastructure wave, from power to cooling, in a free report you can grab here).
Micron has also signed 16 Strategic Customer Agreements covering roughly 25% of revenue over their term, with cumulative minimum-price RPO of approximately $100 billion.
MU trades at a trailing PE of 21x and a forward PE of 6x, with the average analyst target at $1,513.11.
Consensus points to fiscal 2027 EPS of $156.0661 on revenue of $244.46 billion, up from a fiscal 2026 EPS estimate of $73.4388.
Memory is the most cyclical corner of semiconductors, and the 2027 pricing backdrop the new module lands into cannot be forecast from here. That is the case for restraint even after a record quarter.
The bull case rests on structural supply tightness, take-or-pay SCAs whose floor prices Mehrotra said support margins “significantly above prior peak margins”, and Micron’s status as the only US-based memory maker. If HBM4E and the 512GB RDIMM arrive into a still-tight 2027, earnings power expands again.
The bear case is cycle math. At a market cap near $1.1 trillion, MU already discounts continued industry discipline, and the density milestone will not rescue the stock if DRAM prices roll over before qualification revenue shows up.

67,2026-09-18,"Snowflake (SNOW) has returned about 53% over the past year, and its product revenue growth has accelerated for three straight quarters. In early September, the company cut its fiscal 2027 non-GAAP product gross margin guide by a point, to 74%, pointing to a heavier mix of fast-growing AI workloads that carry a lower contribution margin. So what happens to the stock if that mix keeps shifting?
Is Snowflake Already Paying For Its AI Growth?
It has started to. On its fiscal Q1 2027 call in May, the CFO said Snowflake’s AI products carry a lower gross margin than the core platform, and that lower bandwidth costs under an AWS contract were paying for the difference. By the September call, the drag was showing up in the guide anyway.
Management puts Cortex Code, CoWork and the other AI products at about half of the acceleration. Cortex Code added more than 2,000 net new accounts in fiscal Q2 2027 to pass 9,100 in total, while CoWork reached 5,800 accounts. The faster those products land, the more of the revenue sits at the lower margin.
What Would Another Point Of Margin Cost Snowflake?
Less than the guide cut makes it sound. Management guides fiscal 2027 product revenue to about $6.07 billion, so one point of product gross margin is worth roughly $61 million a year. Snowflake’s market value is about $118 billion against $5.4 billion of total revenue over the past twelve months, close to 22 times sales. A point of gross margin does not move a stock priced like that.
What moves it is whether the cost comes back lower down. On the same day, the fiscal 2027 non-GAAP operating margin guide went the other way, up to 14.5% from 13.5%: AI mix costs gross margin, slower headcount spending adds more back below it, and the operating line is the one the verdict rests on. Snowflake is not profitable on a GAAP basis yet, so the offset has to keep coming.
How Would You Know Snowflake Is Not Covering The Cost?
Snowflake has cushions, but they are levers, not guarantees. The company ended fiscal Q2 2027 with $4.3 billion of cash and investments against $2.8 billion of total debt, and it affirmed a 23% non-GAAP adjusted free cash flow margin guide for fiscal 2027.
Read the fiscal Q3 2027 guide of 15.5% non-GAAP operating margin, one quarter of the 14.5% full-year guide, against the lowered fiscal 2027 non-GAAP product gross margin guide, and watch whether management still says it is on track for GAAP profitability in fiscal Q4 2028. If gross margin slips again while the non-GAAP operating margin guide holds, Snowflake is still converting the mix into margin. If both slip, Snowflake is buying its growth.
That does not make the wait comfortable. Snowflake has already fallen about 56% peak to trough inside the past three years, and it is priced for the mix to keep working. If that is the part you could not sit through, our drawdown defenders screen ranks the stocks that gave back the least when markets turned.
Would One Scenario Like This Change Your Whole Year?
A what-if is worth thinking through for any stock you own, and worth losing sleep over only for the one you hold too much of. Concentration tends to arrive by accident rather than by decision. What your largest position would do to your net worth is exactly what the Trefis Wealth team computes, with the same rules-based systematic discipline that runs our High Quality Portfolio. 69,2026-09-18,"If you have been reading the news in the last couple of months, probably you saw what is happening regarding the RAMmageddon, with memory prices going up given the shortage caused because the AI companies are buying massive loads of them.
Trying to shield the PC Market from this shortage, Samsung Electronics has decided to outsource the expanded production of its DDR5 memory modules and solid-state drives (SSDs) to third-party manufacturing partners across Asia. This strategic shift allows the South Korean semiconductor manufacturer to concentrate domestic factory capacity primarily on high-bandwidth memory (HBM) tailored for artificial intelligence workloads. 
Domestically, Samsung is retooling its advanced packaging facilities located in Cheonan and Onyang. Conventional production lines at these South Korean sites are being converted into advanced packaging setups dedicated specifically to high-density HBM fabrication. In addition to reallocating existing infrastructure, Samsung has initiated construction on a dedicated HBM fabrication facility at the Onyang campus, backed by an estimated 6 trillion won investment. Concurrently, a $1.5 billion processing plant currently under development in Thai Nguyen province, Vietnam, will be reserved exclusively for testing legacy memory formats, including DDR4, LPDDR4, NAND flash, and UFS storage. 
To offset the domestic transition, Samsung is scaling outsourced operations across key regional partners: 
The strategic transition capitalizes on distinct technical requirements between product categories. Standard DDR5 memory modules rely on surface-mount technology (SMT), a mature and less complex process that third-party packaging and testing subcontractors can readily execute at scale. In contrast, next-generation HBM architectures demand specialized thermal control and vertical packaging techniques requiring dedicated in-house resources. 
The realignment enables Samsung to secure manufacturing yields for upcoming high-bandwidth developments, including future HBM generations, while maintaining global supply for consumer-grade DDR5 and SSD components. In theory, this won’t make the memory prices go down but, at least, it will make it harder for them to go up, since the supply would going to be able to follow the demand.
Filed in Cellphones >Computers >Tablets. Read more about  AI (Artificial Intelligence), RAMmageddon and Samsung. "
70,2026-09-18,"To understand the iPhone Duo and its ability to turn sceptics into believers, you could do a whole lot worse than crib from the wisdom of Jay-Z. Sure, Steve Jobs was a noted fan of Picasso’s “good artists copy, great artists steal”. In this case, Hov called it better with “you made it a hot line, I made it a hot song”.
The point? Folding phones have been around for almost eight years now. A few of them are seriously accomplished devices in their own right. None have the ability to blow up your timeline, bag a spot on the News At Ten and compel a succession of distant acquaintances to slide into my DMs asking some variation of, “So do I have to spend £2,000 on an iPhone now?”
Until the Duo’s 23 October release date, I can’t give you a definitive answer to that question. Having spent a good few hours last week with Apple’s first foldable and the people who made it, I’ve got a pretty good idea of why this device is already a phenomenon. “This is why we do what we do,” says Apple CEO, John Ternus. “It’s really special because it provided an opportunity to just think all-new.”
Apple
Apple
I’ve reviewed a lot of new tech over the past decade or so, and the ideas that tend to stick are almost always some variation on a theme you’re already familiar with. That’s why the Apple Watch managed to usurp a vast swathe of Casios and G-Shocks overnight, while the Vision Pro has struggled to convince folks that strapping a computer to your face is something worth doing on the regular. So rather than emulate the existing, widely-held aesthetic for foldables – tall and thin for as big an inner screen as possible – Molly Anderson, VP of Industrial Design at Apple, sought to lean on a more traditional form factor.
“Everyone knows what a notebook feels like in the pocket and what it feels like to carry around,” she says. “So that was a big part of the inspiration: ‘How do you get to something that gives you space but also feels compact?’”
If you want to get technical, the Duo’s proportions are derived from the international A-Series paper system, so that they remain the same when opened and closed, helping to deliver the thinnest-ever iPhone. And while I’m not sure that its curved titanium frame will hold up to the same abuse as your average Moleskine, there’s an undeniable tactility to this phone that feels intuitive (so long as you hold it right-handed). That sensation is only elevated by a Duo-specific version of iOS that existing iPhone owners can grasp from the off. Even when splitting apps across screens and half-opening it into an alarm clock mode – the whole operating system dynamically adjusts to how the phone is shaped in your hands.
""There was this concept the whole time, ‘How do you feel like you're expanding the device?’” says Steve Lemay, VP of Human Interface Design at Apple. “We didn't want it to feel like you're starting a different operating system when you unfold it. And you're never searching for the button you were just looking at.”
There’s an old cliché about first-gen Apple products that they take a couple of generations to really find their feet. It doesn’t really hold up in practice. The AirPods? They were great straight out of the gate. Same for the iPod. The iPhone Duo could well belong to the same lineage. On first impressions, it feels like the result of patient iteration, not a product in search of an identity. You can see this most immediately in the animation that plays when opening the Duo, where the effect is like peering through a window.
“It was really important to us from the beginning that iPhone Duo feels like one cohesive experience, not two different products awkwardly fused together,” says Lemay. ""...You basically feel like you're peeking through to the inner display, and it just grows underneath you.”
As much as the Duo’s sweep between screens is impressive, it’s this phone’s more subtle refinements that left a lasting impression on me. The way the matte “nano texture” on its inner screen eliminates the crease across its centre without obscuring your viewing experience. The near-imperceptible selfie camera that’s hidden under the same display. And the oh-so-satisfying snap when you clasp it open and shut.
""We really obsessed about how it feels when you open it – it’s incredibly smooth,” says Anderson. “The hinge is defined by a custom variable torque profile, so it’s easy to open, and then it's actually biased so that it holds itself open and lays flat.”
You might not have noticed, but Apple has been soft launching the iPhone Duo’s foundations for some time now. Just as iOS’ recent Liquid Glass redesign was inspired by the Vision Pro’s floating apps and glossy aesthetic, so the Duo’s fluid interface elegantly builds on the same ideals. It’s a foldable that will feel familiar to anyone who has used an iPhone in the last decade or so. Only layered on top of the usual swipes and gestures is a new array of controls, such as tapping the centre of the screen to link two apps together and access them in unison later.
“You'll see a lot of learnings from iPad in here, learnings from even Macintosh, and learnings from the existing phones,” says Lemay. “It's sort of the greatest hits of those products together.”
Unlike most new Apple products, the iPhone Duo won’t ask you to scramble around in search of your app essentials on Day One. If they work on iPhone, they’ll be fine for the Duo. Better still, the likes of Netflix, Slack and Zoom have already been optimised for Apple’s foldable ahead of its release. So you can sit the thing halfway open on your desk and crunch through The Gentleman season two in an afternoon without drawing an iPad mini-sized screen’s worth of attention to yourself, or reply to your boss’ DMs with even greater efficiency. And this is just the beginning for Duo.
Just as you couldn’t swim with the OG Watch and the AirPods only got eartips three years after their release, this device will evolve according to how it's used. For Apple’s design team, that’s part of the appeal of making it.
“There are so many things the developers will come up with that we've never anticipated,” says Lemay. “Some of those will become big ideas that take over the industry and change the product in interesting ways.”
None of this is to say the Duo will live up to the hype. There’s plenty you can nitpick here, from its use of a Touch ID fingerprint sensor over Face ID to a two-lens camera system that’ll likely be outshone by the iPhone 18 Pro’s larger zoom and more capable sensors. This is a different product to Apple’s impractically thin 2015 MacBook and its single USB-C port, but anyone pre-ordering this iPhone on 16 October will be paying £2,000 for a device whose compromises aren’t an issue on “normal” smartphones.
Until now, this is the defining reason why foldables haven’t taken off in a major way, and why plenty of companies aside from Apple are hoping it can breathe new life into the category. So the iPhone Duo’s appeal isn’t rooted in novelty or raw power, even though it has plenty of both. It’s about the little rush you get when opening it up for the first time, and ensuring that holds true for the long run. “Steve talked about the intersection of technology and liberal arts, and I think iPhone Duo is a perfect example of that,” says Ternus.
If you wanted to surmise the Duo with another Jay-Z maxim, it’d be this: “I smarten up, open the market up”.
The iPhone Duo is available for £1,999. Pre-orders begin at 1pm on 16 October. At apple.com

Samsung Galaxy Z Flip 8
Samsung was the number one smartphone brand globally in terms of shipments in Q1 2026 as well as Q2 2026. With the company leading the market in both quarters, it was only logical that it would also come out on top for H1 2026. We now finally have some numbers to confirm that.

Counterpoint Research has published an analysis of the global smartphone market for H1 2026. According to the report, Samsung held a 22% market share, the highest among all brands, putting it in first place. That’s despite the tech giant raising the prices of its phones multiple times.

Counterpoint Research

Counterpoint Research Global Smartphone Market Share H1 2026

The brand managed to grow despite the broader market downturn, which is a notable achievement.

Apple followed closely behind Samsung with a 21% market share. Xiaomi, Oppo, and Vivo occupied the third, fourth, and fifth spots, respectively, with market shares of 11%, 10%, and 8%. With the Galaxy Z Fold 8 series performing exceptionally well, the South Korean tech giant could carry this momentum into H2 2026.

78,2026-09-18,"Nick Gordon here. Sherman Lin, chair of the Taiwan Stock Exchange Corporation, took a little while to get going when I talked to him on a Zoom call over the summer. But he got animated when I asked him what makes Taiwan’s stock market different from, say, exchanges in Singapore and Hong Kong.
“The best way to understand Taiwan is as a technology island,” he said, with an excited smile, before rattling off cities all along Taiwan’s western coast. “We’re like an industrial park…Technology is really in our DNA.”
Lin should be having a good year: Taiwan became the world’s fifth largest stock market by total value in May, behind just the U.S., mainland China, Japan and Hong Kong, according to Bloomberg calculations. But the Taiwan equity market story is, at its core, a TSMC story: The world’s largest chipmaker makes up 40% of the TAIEX, the island’s benchmark stock index.
The TAIEX is up by about 55% for the year so far; TSMC is up by 50%.
That’s why Lin and his colleagues are trying to broaden Taiwan’s appeal, beyond just TSMC to other companies in the AI supply chain, as well as “hidden champions,” profitable companies in sectors that may get overlooked by more electronics-focused investors. 
Taiwan’s stock exchange is also pushing companies to strengthen their corporate governance through its “Power Up” program, following in the footsteps of Japan and South Korea. Japan’s drive to get its companies to take shareholder value more seriously—through more transparent disclosures, encouraging share buybacks, and unwinding its complicated cross-shareholding structures—has helped lift its stock market to record highs. 
There’s still a long way to go, though: Taiwan raised $3.3 billion over 70 IPOs last year. That was a record for the island’s stock exchanges, but it’s far below Hong Kong, which raised $37.4 billion across 119 deals over the same period.
Taiwan’s stock market can be out of step with the rest of the world, time-wise. For example, the market closes for trading at 1:30 PM. Lin had previously suggested expanding the exchange’s trading hours, only for Taiwan’s top regulator to knock down the suggestion as “not a priority.”
Still, Lin sees the rise of AI, which has lifted valuations for chipmakers and other hardware companies across all of Asia, as an opportunity for Taiwan to lift its profile—one that might run its course in just three years.  
“TSMC is undoubtedly Taiwan’s most iconic company,” Lin said. “But our real competitive advantage is not that we have one or two world-class companies. It’s that we have the world’s most complete and competitive AI ecosystem.”
See you next week,

80,2026-09-18,"Mellow Sleep melds affordability with truly innovative bedding products. They've also created quite a presence on social media, namely TikTok, where, after interacting with a single video, a Live or sponsored post seems to follow me every few swipes. Oh, and the brand is almost always advertising a sale with deep discounts of up to 50%.
Perhaps Mellow Sleep is most famous for its CloudAlign pillow, an ergonomically shaped foam pillow, and MarshMellow comforter, a double-stuffed comforter that promises never to go flat. The pricing is affordable, and there almost always seems to be a sale. I was intrigued to try to see how these products fit in with the likes of the best pillows and comforters. After spending a month with five of Mellow's popular products, I've developed a few favorites and spotlight everything you should know about the brand before making a purchase. I read the fine print, so you don't have to.
Mellow Sleep is a direct-to-consumer bedding brand founded in 2025 that combines affordability with innovative, cooling, and ergonomically designed sleep products. Fueled by a viral TikTok Shop presence (including targeted marketing, sponsored posts, and live streams), the company reported scaling to $110 million in revenue within its first nine months. Product design at Mellow is heavily driven by customer social media feedback and online reviews to address everyday sleep discomfort. Mellow also operates an Amazon storefront, but the prices are better when shopping directly.
However, its digital storefront reflects that rapid growth: navigating the website feels unpolished, offering an endless scroll of products rather than clean, filterable categories. I'm a big proponent of user-experience, so I hope the brand will consider adding filters to its browsing experience. The site does a good job of marketing its bundles to offer more savings.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
What we like
What we don't like
The Mellow CloudAlign Pillow lives up to its name in both shape and softness, but it's far more than just a visual gimmick. It quickly became my favorite model in the Mellow lineup thanks to its cushy, supportive feel and impressive heat dissipation. Sizing is based on user height rather than standard or king-size dimensions: Regular for under 5'7"" and High for over 5'7"".
Ergonomically, the design shines across different sleep styles to combat neck pain. As a back sleeper, my head naturally centered in the middle contour, while I felt excellent neck support from the raised edges I nuzzled into on either side. It also includes dedicated arm-cradle grooves along the sides, giving side and stomach sleepers a natural spot to plant their arms, reducing shoulder compression and improving circulation. It features a dual-edge setup dubbed the ""Soft Side"" and ""Lift Side,"" allowing you to switch between subtle levels of neck elevation depending on how you sleep, but I didn't notice any visual differences until I read the specifications.
I also encouraged my deputy editor, Bronwyn Barnes, who is primarily a side sleeper and a big fan of crescent-shaped pillows, to try the pillow, but we quickly realized the CloudAlign is not for everyone. She found the solid foam core design too ""technical"" and didn't match how she likes to curl up with a pillow, which involves tucking her arm under the pillow, not beside it. ""Because of the specific cutouts and zones, I had to consciously negotiate my body into the right position around the pillow's soft contours and folds every time I moved during the night.""
My other downside is the initial off-gassing smell, which took about a full week to completely clear out. Additionally, because the foam core cannot be machine-washed, using a cover is essential. Mellow sells a cooling cover add-on that arrives pre-installed, which hugs its contours well and fits inside a standard pillowcase, though the slick cooling fabric can cause the pillow to slide around inside traditional cases. Once aired out, however, its stable cushioning and overall comfort make it a standout choice.
What we like
What we don't like
I'm typically skeptical of cooling comforters, but the CloudCool is a true contender for the best cooling comforters. This one has a dual-sided design: an icy-cool ""Arctic Silk"" side and a reverse-breathable ""Cloud Knit."" Slipping underneath the ArcticSilk layer, I immediately felt a cooling sensation that remained throughout the night. I even felt like it left my feet cold. The Cloud Knit side is easy to reverse and has that better, more matte look I prefer for my bedroom aesthetic. It's not cold to the touch, but breathable, should you want to switch up the feeling.
My one issue with the comforter is that it's described as ""plush."" I found that it follows the thin layers of most of the cooling comforters I've come across. It lacks the loftiness of a traditional comforter; it more reminds me of an oversize throw blanket.
This is also where I started to notice and question the lack of material transparency, where online, the materials are simply listed as ""OEKO-TEX® Certified: Hypoallergenic, safe for sensitive skin, and responsibly made with ethical and eco-friendly processes."" You have to reach for the care tag directly; the silky cooling layer is nylon and spandex, while the cloud knit is polyester and spandex.
What we like
What we don't like
If you want a thick, ultra-puffy comforter on a budget, the MarshMellow is a standout option. True to its ""double-stuffed"" description, it hasn't flattened, clumped, or developed cold spots after a full month of testing. The blanket itself is very generously sized.
It's filled with a proprietary fabric it calls Naia, described as a sustainable, cruelty-free, vegan microfiber blend that mimics the plush loft of down and is heat-adaptive, but it doesn't go into much more detail. Again, this is where I had to reach for the care tag to find that the fill is 100% polyester and dig through blog posts to learn more about the material. Rather than loose fill, the interior feels like a solid webbed layer. My biggest trade-off here is that it leaves the blanket a little floppy overall, rather than drapey. It kind of juts out over the edge of my bed, rather than pooling over.
Testing this comforter in peak summer revealed that it runs warm, making it an excellent heavy layer for fall and winter rather than an all-season blanket. It comes in 13 colors and features built-in corner loops for securing a duvet cover. Using a cover is likely the best route; while the outer fabric is soft against the skin, it feels prone to long-term wear and pilling. Finally, keep care requirements in mind — while heavily advertised as machine washable, the fine print specifies that home washing only applies to Twin sizes. Anything larger will require a trip to the laundromat for commercial-sized machines.

                                The Puff Pillow features a dual-layer design, including a visible grid layer that's plush and airy, with a mushy, sink-in feel that bounces back without requiring re-fluffing. 
                              
What we like
What we don't like
What we like
What we don't like
The Mellow Puff is delightfully plush and airy. It features a unique design I haven't seen before: a series of mini squares stitched onto one side that the brand calls a ""puff grid."" This top layer has a soft, sink-in quality, and while it is generously overfilled, it offers minimal support for back and side sleepers. A slightly denser base layer keeps the pillow from going completely flat, but I still found myself craving more structural support. It's undeniably cozy to squeeze and curl up with, but it wouldn't be my first choice as a standalone pillow unless paired with a secondary cushion to provide the necessary loft.
The outer shell is a stretchy, cool-to-the-touch polyester-and-spandex blend, filled with a down-alternative fill that enhances its airy feel. The cooling-cover fabric upper is available in several colors, making the decorative puff grid look almost like an accent sham, while also juxtaposing it against the standard white base. The cooling cover feels refreshingly smooth against the skin, and you might be tempted to skip a pillowcase to show off the color. The cover is non-removable, meaning sleeping directly on it requires washing the entire pillow more frequently.
The Adapt Pillow is the down-alternative counterpart to the CloudAlign, but I immediately found its loft too low and its fill too dense. I didn't survive longer than a nap with this one. The pillow is described as having multi-zoned support, with special spots for side sleeping, head relief, and two cervical spine relief zones. I found the pillow too flat and best suited to stomach sleepers.
The cover is OEKO-TEX® Certified, but the exact fabric is not listed online; the label reads polyester. It is machine washable, however.
Mellow Sleep occupies a specific niche in the bedding market: accessible pricing paired with clever, highly functional designs. The novel ergonomics and active cooling features deliver instant comfort that punches above its price point.
However, the brand falls short on transparency. Product descriptions lean heavily on marketing jargon and proprietary blend names—like ""Naia"" or unnamed down-alternatives—without clearly disclosing the actual root fibers or foam compositions. A mention of being approved by chiropractic partners fails to specify any endorsing bodies outright. And if that information is there, it is heavily buried.
While several Mellow product listings advertise OEKO-TEX certification, only the CloudCool Comforter had a physical care tag, and scanning its QR code revealed that the certificate had expired. OEKO-TEX STANDARD 100 guarantees that every component, from fabrics and dyes to internal fillings, is tested for over 1,000 harmful chemicals, heavy metals, and allergens. An expired tag means there is no active third-party verification that the current production still adheres to those strict standards.
When I reached out to the brand, they informed me that the certificate is current, but the production batch links to a previous certificate, and the renewed certificate has a new code.
There's also no mention of CERTIPUR-US- certified foam or foam densities, both common attributes typically disclosed for these types of products. I did find a mention about its use of CERTIPUR-US foams buried in a blog post, but no markings on the actual product listing or product.
Additionally, details surrounding warranty claims, return policies, and even care remain somewhat vague and buried in the fine print. While you have a 30-day trial, returns are not free.
Mellow is a great entry-level choice if you want trend-forward, cooling bedding on a budget, but temper your expectations if you're looking for natural materials or ""buy it for life"" quality.
Mellow offers a 30-day return window from delivery for items in like-new condition. Returns incur a $15 restocking fee and must be returned in original packaging. If you choose to shop through Amazon, you can take advantage of free returns. Mellow Sleep also offers a ""lifetime warranty"" at $20 per item at checkout that covers manufacturing defects and covers one replacement item. However, there's some fine print to be wary of, including a $15 processing fee per claim, and you must return the item in its original packaging; otherwise, the claim is denied.
Twin sizes fit standard household washers, but Queen and King sizes require commercial laundromat machines due to bulk.
For blankets, the CloudCool Cooling Comforter uses ice-silk cooling fabric and breathable fabrics, whereas the MarshMellow Comforter traps more warmth with its higher fill weight (500 grams versus 2500 grams). None of the pillows we tested trapped heat or caused heat issues.
Naia is the filling Mellow uses for its comforters. Mellow explains that the biodegradable fiber combines softness, breathability, and moisture management while maintaining a low environmental footprint. The OEKO-TEX Standard tag I scanned said the proprietary fill material, Naia, is cellulose acetate fiber (derived from wood pulp), but the care tag simply says it's polyester. Unfortunately, the label I scanned says the certificate has expired, but Mellow reps said the certificate has been renewed under a different number.

81,2026-09-18,"Two days after the Senate killed crypto legislation, the CFTC quietly filed a rulemaking with the White House that could reshape how exchanges list XRP and Bitcoin. The filing is real, but what it can legally do stops well short…
It sounds nuts, but SoFi1 is giving new Active Invest users up to $1,000 in stock for a limited time, and all it takes is a $50 deposit to get started.2 See for yourself (Sponsor)
This post may contain links from our sponsors and affiliates, and Flywheel Publishing may receive compensation for actions taken through them.
On September 17, 2026, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission submitted its rulemaking for the crypto market to the White House Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs, just two days after the Senate rejected the CLARITY Act by a narrow vote of 49 to 50. The filing is identified on Reginfo.gov as RIN 3038-AF80, titled Regulation of Crypto Asset Transactions and Crypto Asset Markets, and its contents remain confidential while under review.
Chairman Michael Selig indicated on the day of the Senate vote that the agency was “locked in and ready to ship rules.” Just two days later, he delivered the filing. So, what does this entry reveal, and how quickly could it impact exchanges listing assets like XRP (CRYPTO:XRP) or Bitcoin (CRYPTO:BTC)?
blackred / Getty Images
The entry shows a receipt date of September 17, a pre-rule stage, no economic significance, and no legal deadlines. It confirms Dodd-Frank authority and identifies no international impacts.
Prerule signifies the stage before a proposed rule, meaning the CFTC has notified the White House of its intention to draft one. The document heading to publication is an advance notice and is not something that exchanges can currently comply with.
The absence of economic significance indicates that the agency anticipates no annual impact of $100 million or more, which may expedite the review process. By flagging Dodd-Frank, the CFTC is invoking the 2010 statute governing leveraged, margined, and derivatives-style trading.
The rule text is confidential until the review concludes, leaving only the title to hint at its scope. It combines Crypto Asset Transactions, which refers to trade, custody, and settlement processes, with Crypto Asset Markets, which pertains to the structuring and registration of trading venues. This two-part division is similar to what the CLARITY Act aimed to establish.
U-STUDIOGRAPHY DD59 / Shutterstock.com
In a speech from August 2026, Selig outlined a plan to direct his staff to explore rules that would “codify a CFTC market structure for crypto assets using the agency’s existing authorities.”
Selig intends to create a registration category that does not exist yet. “This could enable current registrants as well as non-registrant crypto exchanges to be designated by the CFTC as a type of DCM known as a crypto asset market and offer crypto asset trading on a leveraged or margined basis subject to purpose-fit rules under the CFTC’s regulatory oversight,” he said.
A DCM is a designated contract market, the CFTC’s term for an exchange it oversees, and any exchange holding that designation must adhere to 23 core principles outlined in Section 5(d) of the Commodity Exchange Act.
Selig was confirmed as the agency’s 15th chairman on December 18, 2025, and his staff issued a separate no-action letter on September 17 freeing passive software providers, including wallets and trading apps, from registering as introducing brokers under ten conditions.
Visuals6x / Shutterstock.com
Executive Order 12866 gives the White House up to 90 days to review, with a one-time extension of up to 30 days. Filings deemed not economically significant often clear the review process more quickly, although reviewers may send the entire document back to the agency for revisions.
Once the White House review is complete, the Commission will vote to release the advance notice, publish it in the Federal Register, and initiate a comment period typically lasting 60 days.
Afterward, the CFTC will draft a proposed rule, resubmit it to the White House, hold another vote, publish it again, and open a second comment period. The final rule will follow the same review process before it takes effect.
If the review wraps up within 60 days, publication could occur in November or December 2026, with a proposed rule emerging in 2027, and a final binding rule potentially not in effect until late that year.
Virrage Images / Shutterstock.com
The Commodity Futures Trading Commission regulates commodities, and XRP was classified as one in March. On March 17, the joint interpretation by the SEC and CFTC identified 18 digital assets as digital commodities, including Bitcoin, Ether (CRYPTO:ETH), Solana (CRYPTO:SOL) and XRP.
Of the 18 assets, 16 already have futures contracts trading on CFTC-regulated exchanges, while the other two do not, which is why many reports focused on the smaller number. This rulemaking could establish a working framework that includes registration, custody, and trading rules, and that distinction carries weight for XRP, because statutes remain effective despite changes in administration, and regulations do not.
A future Commission could alter a rule, and anyone with standing can challenge it in court under the Administrative Procedure Act. JPMorgan analysts highlighted this concern on September 16, noting that agency rules are less stable than legislation since they can be modified or contested.
Ripple CEO Brad Garlinghouse urged Atkins and Selig to act after the vote, indicating Ripple’s acceptance of the administrative process, despite its limitations. Bitcoin, on the other hand, did not require this classification, so the rule changes its market structure instead of its status. Trading venues could offer leveraged Bitcoin trading under the CFTC’s oversight, with terms set by the agency, which is what institutions sought from the bill.
However, the rule cannot grant the CFTC full authority over the spot market for digital commodities, as that was central to the CLARITY Act and would necessitate an act of Congress.
The CFTC acted within two days on an issue that the Senate took two years to address. Yet, this action is just the beginning of a process that involves two comment periods and two additional reviews by the White House. The text of the proposal is confidential and currently in the prerule stage, and the earliest a binding rule might take effect is late 2027.
So, while faster than congressional action, this process is still considerably slower than headlines may suggest. If the review is completed by December, the public will see the scope of the proposal this year, with a formal proposed rule to follow in 2027.
For both XRP and Bitcoin, this framework would introduce registration, custody, and trading rules around a classification that now relies solely on a single interpretive document. When the proposal is published, the critical question will be whether Selig’s crypto asset market category remains within the agency’s authority for leveraged trading or extends to the spot market, as that expansion was initially expected to require Congressional action.

82,2026-09-18,"Yesterday was the four-year anniversary of my iPhone Xs Max Exit Review. I gave it a read this morning since I may be on the verge of writing a new iPhone exit review (👀) and one of my footnotes caught my attention.
""Just glancing at MKBHD’s top 20 videos (out of 1,464), seven of them are iPhone specific. They total a cool 84 million views. Also, his most viewed video is about the Game Boy with 39 million views. The second most viewed has 23 million and is about the first Samsung Fold.""
I was curious how much the top 20 had changed in four years. Color me surprised that a one-week-old video entered the top three.12

I think the iPhone Duo is going to do just fine.
Looking a bit deeper at the top 20, half of the videos are related to Apple with seven being focused on the iPhone. Those seven iPhone videos tally up to 118 million views, which is about a 40 percent increase in four years. Out of those seven iPhone videos, five of them have come out within the last four years. Here they are from lowest to highest view count:
The poor iPhone 16 is sitting in 31st place... What this little list tells me though is that the immediate impressions after the event are far more popular than the review a week or so later. The fervor of the September iPhone event is unstoppable. It makes me wonder how popular the assumed spring event for the non-Pro iPhones will be in comparison.
When looking at the rest of the top 20 lineup, two are about the Apple Vision Pro, four are Tesla-focused, and three are foldable-focused. Here are the folding videos, bottom to top:
Besides the Duo, there are no videos about the folds or flips in the top 20 since Samsung's foray into the form factor seven years ago. The two non-Duo videos are an unboxing and an explanation about the plastic screen layer that killed the Galaxy Fold if peeled off.3
Scrolling way down the list, we see where folding phones fall in the top video hierarchy. The next folding phone video is about the iPhone Duo. That's right. A follow-up video on the Duo from four days ago is sitting at slot 36. Just two slots later is a video on the folding phone you all love—Royole FlexPai! After a sketchy Pablo Escobar phone and the Galaxy Fold 1.1, we finally get a non-Chinese, non-Samsung folding phone in slot 119 with the Moto RAZR. Google enters the list at 140.
Apple enters the category and in a week it blows past every other folding video.
Now, this doesn't translate to sales and folding phones taking over the entire smartphone market, but it does highlight a few things to me about tech and the viewers of Marques' channel.
Views on YouTube are not direct sales though. They are a live pulse on curiosity and popularity. While I do expect the Duo to sell out instantly, I wager that's more early adopters and speculated low production numbers. John Gruber pondered the form factor's mass appeal:
""Given the design brief of creating a book-shaped foldable phone, I feel quite certain already that Apple has nailed it with the Duo. But having only seen the keynote spiel and poked around with one in hand for a few minutes Wednesday afternoon, I think it remains an open question whether a book-shaped foldable phone is a good idea in the first place. Folks from Apple who have actually spent time carrying the Duo seem genuinely enthused. I remain highly skeptical about its appeal for me personally, and somewhat skeptical about its appeal broadly. That’s OK if it’s not suited for me, but is suited for many (but not most) people. No one in the keynote described the iPhone Duo as the future of iPhone, like they did when introducing the iPhone X in 2017. I just wonder if the fundamental idea appeals to all that many people at all. We’ll find out starting in late October.""
When we consider that Apple is actually marketing the Duo, the reactions in the media, and then combine those with Marques' views, I think the Duo will be a hit.
But not more popular than the Game Boy.
For posterity, here are Marques' top 21* videos today. I can't help that YouTube only displays videos three thumbnails wide. ↩
I suspect the Duo impressions video will be in second place before too long. ↩
I completely forgot that Samsung delayed the Fold last minute to address the hyper sensitive display killing the phone. The revision would come out some five months later. ↩

86,2026-09-18,"Arista Networks (ANET) has gained 5.6% over the last five trading days while the S&P 500 added 0.6%. A gap like that pulls buyers in. But the five-day move is not the question. The real one is what owning Arista does to your money every time the market moves, because it travels much further than the index in both directions.
Arista Has Moved Twice As Far As The Market In Both Directions
Arista cushions nothing. On days the S&P 500 rose over the past year, it captured about 212% of the market’s gain. On days the index fell, it absorbed about 212% of the market’s loss.
That is a one-year reading on daily moves, and it can change. Over the past five years, Arista ran 48.8% annualized volatility against 17.2% for the S&P 500, close to three times the index’s swing. That five-year figure covers all days and runs wider than the one-year capture.
Correlation is a different measure. Over those same five years, Arista’s daily moves tracked the index at 0.56, so it shares some of the market’s direction and keeps behavior of its own. Direction is not the problem. Distance is.
So What Is Arista Levered To?
A small set of very large buyers. Arista sells AI fabrics built on Etherlink switches, and management names Microsoft and Meta as its two longest-standing partners. Arista expects one or two customers to each take at least a tenth of revenue.
The parts come from somewhere else. Management has almost tripled its multiyear purchase commitments over the past year, to about $9.7 billion at the end of fiscal Q2 2026. Even so, the CEO says the industry does not get clear of its supply shortage until 2028.
So the swing has a source. Demand sits with a very small number of customers, supply sits with an industry shortage Arista does not control, and the stock trades at 62.2 times earnings against an S&P 500 median of 22.5. None of that is a flaw. It is what a direct bet on AI build-out looks like.
Have You Been Paid For The Swing?
So far, yes. Over the past five years, Arista returned 54.8% a year against 13.1% for the S&P 500. Adjusted for risk the edge is much smaller: a five-year Sharpe ratio of 1.07 against 0.57, which measures return above the risk-free rate for each unit of swing. The next stretch depends on whether demand keeps turning into shipments.
Management raised its 2026 revenue guidance for the third time, to $12.6 billion and 40% annual growth. That added $1.1 billion to its May 2026 projection, and management has not split it by product line. It expects every product line to contribute, and says the mix depends on what it can ship.
Arista keeps enough behavior of its own to earn a place on its own merits, and over five years it has paid well for the ride. What it will not do is soften the market’s bad days. What ships from here tells you whether that trade stays worth making.
Is Your Biggest Position Undoing Your Diversification?
Diversification is the rare free lunch in investing, and the hardest part is applying it to a position that has already grown large. Concentration tends to arrive by accident rather than by decision. What your largest position would do to your net worth is exactly what the Trefis Wealth team computes, with the same rules-based systematic discipline that runs our High Quality Portfolio. Request a free vulnerability audit of your biggest positions.

87,2026-09-18,"McDonald's stock just hit a two-year low while the broader market rallied more than 11%, and one Wall Street analyst sees that gap as the setup of the year for patient investors.

Shares of McDonald’s (NYSE:MCD | MCD Price Prediction) trade near $248.48, well below Wall Street’s $312.84 average price target, a gap of roughly 26%.
The world’s largest restaurant chain by system sales rarely shows up in dislocation stories, yet a U.S. execution stumble has pushed the stock to two-year lows even as MCD just marked 50 consecutive years of dividend increases. Truist’s Jake Bartlett carries a $356 target implying roughly 43% upside.
Global comparable sales grew just 1.3% in Q2 fiscal 2026, down from 3.8% a year earlier, with U.S. comps at only 0.8% and traffic negative. EPS of $3.38 beat, but SG&A jumped 17% and U.S. comps were slightly negative in July.
CEO Chris Kempczinski was blunt: “We don’t have a strategy problem. We simply didn’t execute at the level we needed to in the second quarter.” Management blamed inconsistent rollout of the under-$3 value menu, reduced digital offers, and an underperforming FIFA campaign. MCD has fallen from $329.16 at the February earnings report to today’s level, about 26% off the $335.18 52-week high, while the S&P 500 is up 11.85% YTD. This is a name-specific unwind.
Sell-side coverage remains constructive. Of 34 analysts, 4 rate MCD Strong Buy, 15 Buy, 14 Hold, and 1 Sell.
Bartlett’s $356 Buy-rated target rests on four planks. First, the $5 Meal Deal extension and app-only offers pull traffic from regional QSR and casual dining. Second, digital monetization: MCD counts nearly 220 million 90-day active loyalty users and over $40 billion in trailing systemwide sales to that base, with app orders carrying higher tickets and lower labor overhead.
The third plank is international recovery from the Q2 1.5% International Operated Markets comp, with France stabilizing and Japan compounding via loyalty. The fourth is franchisee operating leverage. Kempczinski expects the U.S. to be “fully back to where we need to be in 2027”, with an Investor Day on September 23 in Chicago as the next catalyst.
The QSR peer group did not sell with McDonald’s. Yum! Brands (NYSE:YUM) trades near $135.75 versus a $173.79 target, implying about 28% upside. YUM is off 10.27% YTD, but Q2 was cleaner as Taco Bell posted 7% same-store sales growth. Coverage skews Buy with 2 Strong Buy, 9 Buy, and 15 Hold.
Restaurant Brands International (NYSE:QSR) trades near $73.29 against an $85.92 target, only about 17% upside. QSR is up 7.42% YTD, riding Burger King’s 8.5% U.S. Q2 comp. Ratings run 3 Strong Buy, 14 Buy, 9 Hold, and 1 Strong Sell.
Starbucks (NASDAQ:SBUX) sits near $96.67 versus a $112.23 target, about 16% upside. SBUX is up 14.80% YTD after a 7.9% global comp in fiscal Q3, with coverage split 4 Strong Buy, 12 Buy, 17 Hold, 1 Sell, and 2 Strong Sell.
Largest consensus upside belongs to YUM at 28%, with MCD close behind at 26%. Bartlett’s $356 on MCD is the biggest single-name gap on the board.
MCD currently trades at $248.48, with a 34-analyst average target of $312.84. Shares are off 17.11% YTD and 16.42% over one year, versus the S&P 500’s 11.85% YTD gain.
Valuation looks defensible: a trailing P/E of 20, forward P/E of 18, dividend yield of 2.9%, and free cash flow yield near 4.1%.
McDonald’s works here if U.S. execution issues are fixable within quarters, national value pricing regains awareness, and international markets accelerate off the 1.5% Q2 comp. That path leads to Bartlett’s $356, aided by franchisee alignment, loyalty compounding, and the September 23 Investor Day.
The bear case builds if the low-income consumer stays pressured, the under-$3 menu fails to reset traffic, and China plus France remain drags. Those risks turn a defensive Dividend King into a slow-bleed value trap.
The setup skews cautiously constructive. The dividend is bulletproof, the balance sheet supports buybacks, and the setup pays you to wait for U.S. execution to catch the international book. It may still take through 2027 before shares reach Bartlett’s $356.

88,2026-09-18,"Thursday's rotation rally handed automakers a one-day reprieve from months of pressure, but Friday morning that same trade ran straight into reverse, and the order in which Stellantis, GM, and Ford are falling tells the whole story.
 Market Movers desk. Editor: David Moadel.
The professional research desk has always been the part of Wall Street that retail investors could not buy. AlphaSpace by Yahoo Finance opens one for $39.95 a month, and the first seven days cost nothing.1 (Sponsor)
This post may contain links from our sponsors and affiliates, and Flywheel Publishing may receive compensation for actions taken through them.
Automaker stocks are giving back Thursday’s rotation-driven pop in Friday morning trading, and the order in which the group is falling reads like that same trade running backwards. No company announcement dated today explains the move. Stellantis N.V. (NYSE:STLA | STLA Price Prediction) is leading the group lower, and it’s the most striking illustration of what a category flow does when it arrives without a story.
Stellantis stock is at $4.86, down 4% mid-morning. At the same time, General Motors (NYSE:GM) shares are at $83.21, down 4%, matching Stellantis at the front of the decline. Ford Motor (NYSE:F) stock is at $13.28, down 2%, participating in the same reversal on a smaller scale.
The Consumer Discretionary Select Sector SPDR ETF (NYSEARCA:XLY) is down 0.6%, and Stellantis, General Motors and Ford are all falling faster than that. Meanwhile, the SPDR S&P 500 ETF Trust (NYSEARCA:SPY) is down 0.2%. That stacking, sector under benchmark and automakers under sector, is the mirror image of what the group did on the way up.
On Thursday, the automaker group rallied together on a rotation bid into cyclicals, with no earnings release, guidance change or sales tally attached to the move. A flow of that shape tends to reverse on the same absence of news that created it. That is what Stellantis, Ford and General Motors are doing this morning.
No filing sits on the wire this morning from any of the three. Stellantis’s most recent release, from earlier this week, covered a Dodge motorsports promotion. Ford and General Motors have nothing new on their release feeds either.
The mechanism is simple. Category-level flow buys the label rather than the operator, and when it reverses it hands value back in the same order and at the same relative speed at which it arrived. Stellantis, Ford and General Motors ran ahead of their sector while the trade was on, and they are running behind it now that the trade is unwinding.
That ordering is what makes the setup recognizable. Stellantis, Ford and General Motors are each declining faster than the consumer discretionary fund that holds them, and that fund is falling faster than the broad market fund. Nothing in the pattern points to a company story.
Stellantis and General Motors are running well ahead of the broad market fund’s slip, with Ford close behind. The relative speed of each decline lines up with how each name behaved during Thursday’s rally. Category-flow trades produce that stacking at both ends.
This is the read of a group trade rather than three separate decisions about three different balance sheets. What didn’t change between yesterday’s close and this morning’s open is the underlying business at Stellantis, Ford or General Motors. The flow around the label was doing the buying, and it is doing the selling this morning.
Stellantis stock is down 55% year to date (YTD), a figure that describes a business the rotation bid briefly stopped pricing. Stellantis’s second-quarter revenue landed 41.5% below year-ago levels, and adjusted operating margin sat at 1.8%. A one-day sector bid doesn’t repair any of that, and a one-day sector unwind doesn’t worsen it.
The company did swing back to a $293 million quarterly profit from a $1.87 billion loss a year earlier, and North America has been the clearest operational bright spot. Yet, the YTD decline is what a stock does when the market questions the quality of the recovery. The rotation trade briefly overwrote that question, and this morning it’s back on the screen.
General Motors and Ford enter today’s session with cleaner recent operating stories than Stellantis. However, the same category flow that lifted all three names yesterday is what’s pressing on them today. The mechanism is indifferent to which operator has the stronger quarter behind it.
Investors can watch for whether Stellantis, Ford and General Motors keep outrunning the consumer discretionary fund to the downside through midday. If the three names hold that stacked relationship into the close, the rotation-unwind read holds and today’s decline sits in the flow rather than in the operator.
Traders’ positions in Stellantis, Ford or General Motors should account for how much of any given day’s move belongs to the operator and how much belongs to the group flow around it. Sizing to each company rather than to the full category is the way to keep a rotation unwind from doing more damage than the underlying results warrant.

            Saudi Arabia’s luxury hotel boom is mostly built. Hilton’s bet on what comes next is mid-market brands and franchises — and it has Turkey as proof the playbook works.        
Select a question above or ask something else
Hilton has spent years building luxury hotels in Saudi Arabia. Now its EMEA president says the upper end of the market is largely established, and the next wave of demand will come from mid-market brands and franchises.
“We’ve got Conrads, Waldorfs, Hiltons, Curios, and DoubleTrees coming out of our ears over there,” Simon Vincent, Hilton’s president for Europe, Middle East, and Africa, told Skift. “But it’s the mid-market we want to build out, and that’s how, very often, markets evolve.”
Vincent compared the strategy to Hilton’s development in Turkey: from the Hilton Istanbul Bosphorus, which opened in 1955, to more than 100 hotels in the country today. The build-out came mainly through mid-market brands such as Hilton Garden Inns in provincial towns and Hampton hotels in secondary cities — not more Conrads.

Hilton's EMEA president Simon Vincent told Skift that with the luxury tier in Saudi Arabia and the wider Middle East now largely established, the company's next growth phase will center on mid-market brands (such as Hilton Garden Inn and Hampton) delivered increasingly through franchising. Vincent likens the strategy to Hilton's build-out in Turkey, where growth came from mid-market brands in secondary cities rather than more luxury flagships. This push comes against a difficult regional backdrop: Middle East and Africa RevPAR fell roughly 30% year-on-year in Q2, with full-year regional RevPAR expected to decline high single to low double digits and a direct EBITDA hit of more than $20 million tied to regional conflict. Despite this, developer appetite remains strong — Middle East signings rose in Q2 and Hilton's 70-hotel Saudi pipeline (much of it already under construction) is holding firm even as Vision 2030 giga-projects are scaled back. Vincent notes franchising follows market maturity and brand type — luxury brands like Waldorf Astoria stay under management to protect the customer experience, while more formulaic mid-market and budget properties suit franchising. Europe, meanwhile, is Hilton's strongest-performing region globally, with first-half RevPAR up 5.5%, driven by robust Southern European leisure demand and surprising UK resilience.

92,2026-09-18,"Walt Disney (DIS) has lost about 7% over the past twelve months while the S&P 500 gained 17%. Disney trades at 21.3 times earnings, just under an S&P 500 median of 22.5. The question is whether that is a good business on sale, or a fair price for a slow grower.
What Is Disney Doing Better Than Its Stock Suggests?
Disney sells days at Walt Disney World, cabins on Disney Cruise Line, subscriptions to Disney+ and ESPN, and the characters that carry all of it. In fiscal Q3 2026, that machine ran well: revenue rose 7%, and total segment operating income rose 21%, ahead of management’s own prior guidance. Toy Story 5 passed the $1 billion threshold during its summer run.
The cash behind it is real. Over the past twelve months, Disney turned just under $99 billion of revenue into close to $8.3 billion of free cash flow, positive in every rolling twelve-month period over the last three years. Management then raised its fiscal 2026 buyback, funded largely by cash previously set aside for the OpenAI deal and by expected A&E proceeds. Cash like that, priced a shade below the market median, is what makes value buyers look twice.
So Why Does Disney Still Trade Below The Market?
Widen the window and the case thins. Over the last three years, Disney’s revenue grew 4.0% a year on average, against an S&P 500 median of 8.3% over the past year alone; different windows, but Disney trails on either measure. Disney’s operating margin over the past twelve months was 15.2%, against a market median of 18.6%. Fiscal Q3 2026 is one quarter, and the three-year average is the record the market prices.
The business explains both. Films are lumpy: management concedes that two releases, The Mandalorian and Grogu plus the live-action Moana, missed their box office expectations. In the parks, a weaker consumer in Shanghai and Hong Kong held back fiscal Q3 2026 and still does in fiscal Q4 2026, management says.
A business growing slower than the market and earning less on each dollar of sales does not get a market multiple. The discount is the market pricing Disney, not overlooking it.
Can Disney Grow Its Way Out Of This?
Disney is spending against exactly that. Management is putting about $9 billion of fiscal 2026 capital spending behind Experiences: Villains Land in Orlando, an Avengers Campus expansion in Anaheim, and more Disney Cruise Line ships. Global guests rose 4% in fiscal Q3 2026, before most of that capacity opens.
The test is management’s own bar, set on earnings per share rather than the revenue line above: adjusted earnings per share growth of about 12% for fiscal 2026 excluding an extra week in the fiscal calendar (about 16% including it), and double-digit growth guided for fiscal 2027. Clearing the fiscal 2027 bar would say the buildout is still adding to per-share earnings after a big fiscal 2026. Missing it would say the three-year record was the better guide.
On the evidence, the discount is earned by slower growth, not by a business coming apart. Margins held, cash kept coming, and the guidance that moved, moved up. Our Buy The Dip screen ranks the names that sold off while their businesses held together.
So Should You Buy Disney For The Buildout?
Perhaps, but be clear about which bet you are making. You are not buying a great business at a deep markdown. You are buying a steady business spending heavily to grow faster, and betting the new ships and lands fill up. That is a real thesis, and it is not the same as thinking the stock is cheap. If you would rather not wait for a buildout, look at the Trefis High Quality Portfolio. That portfolio has a track record of outpacing the three major indices.

The car is finished in Pearl Gray Metallic over black leather upholstery and is powered by a 5.0-liter M117 V8 paired with a four-speed automatic transmission. Equipment includes a sunroof, 15″ Gullideckel wheels, a Becker stereo, automatic climate control, a rear sunshade, and heated power-adjustable front and rear seats. The vehicle was featured on BaT in October 2015 and was acquired by the seller on the site in May 2025. This W126 500SEL is now offered at no reserve in California with service records, a Carfax report, and a Montana title in the name of the seller’s LLC.

The car is finished in Pearl Gray Metallic (122) and features a power-operated sunroof, headlight wipers, a power antenna, and bright window and bumper trim. The front bumper, left doors, and left quarter panel were refinished under previous ownership to address paint imperfections. The power adjustment for the right mirror does not work. The parking pole has been removed.

The 15″ Gullideckel alloy wheels are mounted with Vredestein Hitrac All Season tires. Braking is handled by power-assisted four-wheel discs with ABS. Work in July 2025 included performing an alignment, changing the brake fluid and power steering fluid, and replacing the high-pressure power steering hose, lower ball joints, and rear brake pads.

The interior is appointed with black leather upholstery, wood trim, and heated power-adjustable front and rear seats. Details include automatic climate control, cruise control, a Becker stereo, and a rear sunshade. The shifter bushings were replaced in July 2025.

The four-spoke leather-wrapped steering wheel frames a 240-km/h speedometer and a tachometer with a 6k-rpm redline as well as an analog clock and gauges for coolant temperature, oil pressure, fuel level, and instant fuel economy. The six-digit odometer shows 40k kilometers (~25k miles), approximately 1,700 kilometers of which were added under current ownership. A mileage inconsistency is noted in a March 2016 entry on the Carfax report, though it notes that it may be the result of a clerical error.

The 5.0-liter M117 V8 was factory rated at 245 horsepower and 295 lb-ft of torque, which is sent to the rear wheels through a four-speed automatic transmission. The coolant was changed, the water pump was replaced, and the transmission was resealed in July 2025. The differential fluid and engine oil were changed in August 2026, at which time the rear flex disc was replaced.

94,2026-09-18,"By 


      Ricky Mulvey
    

, 


      Wailin Wong
    

The stock market is at record highs, largely thanks to tech stocks that are benefitting from investor optimism about the AI boom. But bond markets may be telling a different story.
MICHEL MARTIN, HOST:  The same big tech companies that are dominating the stock market are starting to reshape the bond market. Our colleagues from The Indicator - Wailin Wong and Ricky Mulvey - looked into the efforts to fund massive AI data centers and the potential risks to 401(k) retirement plans.WAILIN WONG, BYLINE: Data centers are all over the news these days. They are flashpoints in local debates about what should be built in communities.RICKY MULVEY, BYLINE: If you're one of those people who don't want a data center in their backyard, you might want this bond-fueled buildout stopped in its tracks.WONG: Then, of course, if you're rooting for the stock market to keep going up, you need those data centers to power the hopes and dreams and profits of the tech giants. But enough about stocks. I want to talk about bonds.ZACHARY GRIFFITHS: Bonds are interesting.WONG: (Laughter).GRIFFITHS: I mean, that's what we're always saying.WONG: This is fellow bond geek Zachary Griffiths. He works at an independent research firm called CreditSights. His specialty is U.S. investment-grade debt. That refers to safe and highly rated bonds. U.S. Treasurys are investment-grade, and so are bonds from Alphabet, Amazon, Meta and Microsoft.MULVEY: These companies are considered the main hyperscalers. This is a term we didn't really use that much until a few years ago, and now it's getting thrown around all the time.WONG: Yeah. It's come about because it refers to corporations that run huge data centers. These hulking structures take a lot of money to build, and the hyperscalers have a lot of cash.MULVEY: And they're not just wading gently into the bond market. According to Vanguard, the five hyperscalers sold more than $90 billion in bonds last year. And they've already sold more than $130 billion so far in 2026.WONG: And remember, most of the hyperscalers are considered very safe and creditworthy. The bonds they're selling are now competing with U.S. Treasurys for investor dollars. That's helping push up yields for Treasurys.MULVEY: But investors, like hedge funds, pension funds and insurance companies - they don't have unlimited dollars. So we wanted to know - is there enough demand out there for all of these new AI-related bonds?WONG: Is it just this, like, limitless appetite for this stuff? Is it like ""Mean Girls"" - like, the limit does not exist?GRIFFITHS: The limit may not exist, but what does need to shift is how much spread you're getting.WONG: Zachary is talking about the returns that investors are asking for. The higher the risk, the more compensation bondholders want.MULVEY: So is there investor demand? Yes, but with an asterisk.WONG: The second question we want to ask is, how are bondholders for data center projects going to be paid back? Jonathan Mondillo is with Aberdeen Investments.JONATHAN MONDILLO: Each contract seems to be written a little bit different. There's a lack of standardization there. And that's a risk, ultimately.WONG: One risk Jonathan identifies is construction risk. Will the project get done on time? And looking further out, there's a risk of technological obsolescence.MONDILLO: These are long-term lease structures. You know, who's to say that there's need for that amount of space in the data center in 20 years' time?MULVEY: Jonathan talks about lease terms because that is a common arrangement for these data centers. Companies like Meta are planning to rent these buildings rather than own them outright.WONG: These are scenarios that investors have to consider when they're deciding whether to lend money to the hyperscalers - money that they might not make back for decades. And again, if you have savings invested in a bond fund, that's your money at work.Wailin Wong.MULVEY: Ricky Mulvey, NPR News.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)
